                     HOMELANDS AND EMPIRES

        Indigenous Spaces, Imperial Fictions, and Competition
       for Territory in Northeastern North America, 1690–1763




The period from 1690 to 1763 was a time of intense territorial competition during
which Indigenous peoples remained a dominant force. British Nova Scotia and
French Acadia were imaginary places that administrators hoped to graft over
the ancestral homelands of the Mi’kmaq, Wulstukwiuk, Passamaquoddy, and
Abenaki peoples.
  Homelands and Empires is the inaugural volume in the University of Toronto
Press’s Studies in Atlantic Canada History. In this deeply researched and engag-
ingly argued work, Jeffers Lennox reconfigures our general understanding of
how Indigenous peoples, imperial forces, and settlers competed for space in
northeastern North America before the British conquest in 1763. Lennox’s judi-
cious investigation of official correspondence, treaties, newspapers and maga-
zines, diaries, and maps reveals a locally developed system of accommodation
that promoted peaceful interactions but enabled violent reprisals when agree-
ments were broken. This outstanding contribution to scholarship on early North
America questions the nature and practice of imperial expansion in the face of
Indigenous territorial strength.

(Studies in Atlantic Canada History)

jeffers lennox is an assistant professor in the Department of History at Wesleyan
University.
            STUDIES IN ATLANTIC CANADA HISTORY

               Editors: John G. Reid and Peter L. Twohig

This monograph series focuses on the history of Atlantic Canada, inter-
preting the scope of this field in a way that is deliberately inclusive and
accommodating. As well as studies that deal wholly with any aspect
of the history of the Atlantic region (or part thereof), the series extends
to neighbouring geographical areas that are considered in conjunction
with or in parallel with a portion of Atlantic Canada. Atlantic Canada’s
oceanic or global relationships are also included, and studies from any
thematic or historiographical perspective are welcome.

                          Acknowledgments




I’m not sure what people think writing a book looks like. What you’re reading
is the product of many and varied environments: the back of a tour van en route
from Halifax to Toronto; quiet bike rides through southern France, Montreal, Van-
couver, and rural Connecticut; empty parks; more apartments than I care to count;
dinner table conversations; ideas scribbled on scraps of paper; long walks with
Ms Matt Damon; and hours upon hours of conversations with people who are just
scary smart.
   The tradition for acknowledgments is to provide a list of thank yous, and this
tradition is important because the people who made this book what it is mean
much more to me than the book itself. Unfortunately, an exhaustive list of names
is boring. So, in no particular order, I’d like to thank: every archivist I’ve encoun-
tered; all my friends from grad school at Dalhousie who were always up for one
more beer (even when I wasn’t); all my friends outside grad school and aca-
demia who remind me that I don’t have a real job; my bicycles, Indiana Jones and
Benedict Arnold, that were under me when many of the ideas in this book took
shape; students I’ve taught who have asked important questions and forced me to
clarify my arguments; the internet and keyword searchable documents; everyone
at UTP for doing yeoman’s service on this beast; Acadiensis, The Canadian Historical
Review, and The William and Mary Quarterly for allowing me to republish versions
of my articles that first appeared in their journals; people in the seventeenth and
eighteenth centuries for writing things down (even in terrible handwriting); and
last, but not least, the anonymous reviewers who saved me from embarrassing
mistakes and suggested ways to make this book better.
   Some people were closer to this book than others, and they deserve special rec-
ognition. Jerry Bannister, who welcomed me as a “come-from-away” graduate
student hoping to write about David Thompson and the fur trade, was instrumen-
tal in helping me shape this project. He is a model academic who offered excel-
lent advice (on matters scholastic and personal), insightful criticisms, and dinner
parties that at times went on until breakfast. John Reid has been more generous
with his time than any student or early-career academic could possibly expect,
and his encouragement at every stage has meant the world. Elizabeth Mancke,
Matthew Edney, Claire Campbell, Krista Kesselring, Shirley Tillotson, and Danny
Vickers read and commented on early drafts of this project. Len Husband at UTP
believed in this book from day one and always helped me see the bigger picture.
Institutional and governmental largesse from SSHRC, Dalhousie, UBC, Friends
xii                              Acknowledgments


of J.B. Harley, and Wesleyan University sent me to far-flung archives (and kept
the lights on and food in the fridge).
   Those closest to me had the biggest impact on my experience writing and living
with this project. Bradley Miller read parts of this book and offered his usual prob-
ing questions and excellent suggestions. More important, he is a fantastic friend.
My sisters haven’t read anything I’ve written (nor should they), but they always
make me laugh and have always encouraged what I do. My father-in-law, Paul
Marland, has in fact read this entire book. He even had many nice things to say
about it. And Christine, my mother-in-law, never runs out of questions for me to
consider. My parents have supported me in every way imaginable from the time
I was born right up to this very moment and beyond. My father, although retired,
put on his English professor’s hat and spent a recent visit to New Haven proof-
reading the whole damn book while my mum kept an infant and toddler occu-
pied. They are just incredible. I could never repay all the love they have given me,
and I simply won’t repay any of the money.
   This book is dedicated to my little universe. Our newest addition, Rosalind, is
fitting in well mostly because she sleeps and smiles (can’t ask for much more from
an infant), and she arrived just in time to merit a dedication. And Rooke! You’re
only two and a half and you barely comprehend the present, let alone the past.
But one day we’ll go on long bike rides together and talk about life, and I’ll tell
you that when you were small I dedicated a book to you. Rosemary, my wife, is
the reason this book exists. She moved with me to Montreal, then Vancouver, and
then ... gasp! ... the United States. She has consistently amazed me with her abil-
ity to pursue her passions and become more successful at each stop. This journey
would have been intolerable without her and our family traditions. Thank you for
being everything.

           HOMELANDS AND EMPIRES

 Indigenous Spaces, Imperial Fictions, and Competition
for Territory in Northeastern North America, 1690–1763
This page intentionally left blank
                                   Introduction




“The most Christian King shall take care to have delivered to the Queen of Great Britain …
all Nova Scotia or Acadie, with its ancient boundaries.”
                                                           Treaty of Utrecht, Article 12, 1713

“It is God who has given me this land to be my homeland forever.”
                                                Mi’kmaw letter to the British at Halifax, 1749

“Le nom Nouvelle Ecosse étoit un nom en l’air.”
                                  French delegate at the Acadian Boundary Commission, 1750



To talk about Acadia or Nova Scotia in the eighteenth century is to engage in an
act of imagination. Empires were geographic fictions and the colonies that served
as their constituent parts were little more than a collection of pales. Weak forts,
hastily constructed trading posts, and small villages dotted the northeast in a land
that was thoroughly controlled by Indigenous peoples. Larger settlements, such as
Boston, radiated a sense of territorial power that quickly faded as settlers or sol-
diers pushed northeast into the homelands of the Abenaki, Wulstukwiuk, Passa­
maquoddy, and Mi’kmaq. The French, who had lived in these homelands since the
early seventeenth century, managed for the most part to coexist with the Wabanaki
(a term that denotes the informal and fluid alliances that connected Indigenous
nations in the northeast) because French settlers were few in number and more
interested in trade than land appropriation. There was no French version of Boston
in the region from which officials could claim territory. Instead, the administrators
at Port Royal, a modest fort with few offensive or defensive capabilities, oversaw
trade, engaged in diplomacy with Indigenous peoples, and built Acadian settle-
ments, which grew slowly along the Bay of Fundy. They referred to the region as
Acadia, but it wasn’t. This small collection of French settlements was an encroach-
ment barely tolerated by those already living on the land.
   Some places in the northeast were less imagined than others. Indigenous home-
lands existed as lived spaces in which various groups hunted and traded, through
which they travelled and migrated, and from which they developed a sense of
community among and between themselves that was intimately linked to the land
that sustained them. There were three primary homelands in northeastern North
America: Mi’kma’ki was the home of the Mi’kmaw people, who traversed the
4                             Homelands and Empires


peninsula and coastal region; Wulstukwik, named for the Wulstukw River that cut
deep into the continent from the Atlantic shore, supported the Wulstukwiuk and
Passamaquoddy people who lived along that river and its valley; and finally, the
Dawnland, so-called because it was one of the first parts of the continent to wel-
come the sun each day, nourished the Abenaki peoples, who defined their territory
by the waterways and drainage basins that facilitated navigation and offered both
food and shelter. These were the homelands into which European powers intruded.
   British and French officials wanted to subsume these lands – the rivers and
valleys, open fields and woodlands, rocky shores and sandy beaches – into their
geographic fictions called Nova Scotia and Acadia. Referred to variously by impe-
rial officials as plantations, colonies, or overseas possessions, Acadia and Nova
Scotia were imagined places with brief histories and thin populations, placehold-
ers for the aspirations of European powers. But those aspirations were not eas-
ily achieved. Acadia and Nova Scotia existed only as pales: they grew and shrank
over time, ebbing and flowing like the Fundy tides, often seasonal, fleeting, tempo-
rary. They were also dependent on more established European settlements. Acadia
was separate from but linked to Quebec, while Nova Scotia had such a close re-
lationship with Massachusetts Bay that historians have called it “New England’s
outpost” and studied the two places as interconnected.1 Yet, unlike Quebec and
Massachusetts, Acadia and Nova Scotia lacked the military and economic power to
influence their surroundings in a significant way until much later in the eighteenth
century. They were imperial incubators, waiting and hoping that the ideas birthed
in London or Versailles would mature into administrative realities in the northeast.
   This book explores how imperial fictions and Indigenous homelands served
as the site for territorial competition in northeastern North America. It is the
story of European expansion and Indigenous resistance, complicated by the fact
that empires and Indigenous peoples fought both against each other and among
themselves, and struck alliances as necessary. Acadian settlers hoped to maintain
good relations with the Mi’kmaq; the Abenaki endured internal struggles over
whether to ally with the English or French; Mi’kmaw residents signed and then
broke treaties with the British. These specific complications illustrate the complex
relationships that grew up in a region that was forced to define and redefine itself
between 1690 and 1763.
   Of central importance to these conflicts and negotiations was the power of ge-
ography as a language of both conflict and diplomacy, but most of all, empire.
The ability to map a territory – to know its boundaries, describe its topography,
and determine who could live in it or travel through it – served as the basis both
for defending homelands and constructing empires. For imperial officials, map-
ping practices functioned as a process of slow reification: they helped bring to
life the geographic fictions that reflected putative boundaries, the tracts that de-
tailed settlements and agriculture, and the names that reflected a sense of British
or French identity back across the Atlantic. The Wabanaki refused to legitimize
these fictions by supporting the boundaries and internal organization of their home-
lands during times of peace, and by launching military campaigns at times of
war. As territorial and spiritual homelands, Mi’kma’ki, Wulstukwik, and the
Dawnland weakened over the course of the seventeenth and eighteenth cen-
tury, their Indigenous populations reduced by war and disease. Even so, British
                                     Introduction                                   5


and French officials struggled to materialize their imperial fictions. The battle over
Nova Scotia and Acadia took place in northeastern homelands and the region’s
Indigenous population shaped this history, directly and indirectly. The result of
these recurring conflicts demonstrates how the steady growth of European settle-
ments threatened Indigenous peoples and their lands in very real ways, but also
illuminates how thoroughly entangled were the various European and Indigenous
groups living in the region.
   To understand these threats that imperial imaginaries posed, we must first ex-
plore the homelands themselves. The Mi’kmaw people lived in and travelled
through Mi’kma’ki, which corresponds roughly to today’s Nova Scotia, Prince
Edward Island, northern New Brunswick, and the Gaspé peninsula.2 Traditional
accounts state that this region was divided into seven political districts: Kespeke­
waq (Gaspé), Sikniktewaq (New Brunswick and eastern Quebec), Pittukewwaq
aqq Epekwtk (Prince Edward Island), Unama’ki (Cape Breton), Esgigeoaq (north-
ern Nova Scotian peninsula), Sipekne’katik (central peninsula), and Kespu’kwitk
(southern peninsula). However, members of one group seemed to have migrated
into the territory of another with relative ease, and it was not uncommon for sever-
al groups to inhabit the same district. This was a land of lakes, rivers, woods, and
rocky soils, all of which combined to shape the Mi’kmaw existence. They hunted
seal and otters in January, beavers, moose, and caribou inland during the win-
ter months and fished for smelt, herring, sturgeon, and salmon along rivers and
streams in the warmer weather. This semi-nomadic pattern meant that hunting
villages moved regularly, travelling by canoe and on foot.3 As much as the British
or the French, the Mi’kmaq were a maritime people. Over the course of centuries
they had perfected canoe making and water travel, allowing them to include in
their seasonal migrations islands that were hundreds of kilometres offshore. The
Mi’kmaq designed several types of canoes and used each for a distinct purpose:
longer voyagers, faster speeds, and better handling. In the early 1720s, Sebastien
Rale, a Jesuit missionary living with coastal Abenaki, wrote of the Indigenous peo-
ples’ proficiency on water: “It is in these canoes made of bark – which has scarcely
the thickness of an écu – that they cross the arms of the sea, and sail on the most
dangerous rivers, and on lakes from four to five hundred leagues in circumference.
In this manner I have made many voyages, without having run any risk.”4 This
maritime nature made the Mi’kmaq a distinct group among the Wabanaki.5
   Along the western coast of the Bay of Fundy was Wulstukwik, the homeland
of the Wulstukwiuk (whose name translates roughly to “people of the river”) and
Passamaquoddy. These groups spoke similar dialects and shared many of the roll-
ing and forested hills by the Wulstukw River and its tributaries. The Wulstukwiuk
lived along the length of the Wulstukw River, stretching from its mouth at the Bay
of Fundy to the St Lawrence River. The Passamaquoddy, on the other hand, were
more closely packed around Passamaquoddy Bay and up the St Croix River. These
groups fished, planted some corn, and followed game on foot or by canoe over a
vast territory. The Wulstukw River, later called the Saint John, was and remains
an important artery into the continent. It served as the quickest route into Quebec
and was much easier to traverse than the St Lawrence River at any time of year.6
   The Dawnland, home to the Abenaki peoples, including members of the Pequaw­
ket, Arosaguntacook, Kennebec, and Penobscot, was defined by waterways. Its
6                              Homelands and Empires


residents bounded their territory by river basins stretching from the Penobscot
to the Saco, with the highlands of one group bleeding into those of another. The
lands between these rivers did not lend themselves to agriculture, as they were
covered by hardwood and coniferous trees and poor soil, so most Abenaki peoples
fished and hunted like their northern neighbours.7 They were similarly influenced
by European trade goods and religion as missionaries hoped to win souls in the
region.8 Living closer to Boston, the Abenaki’s homeland was reduced faster than
that of the Wulstukwiuk, Passamaquoddy, and Mi’kmaq, but the groups often
found themselves working together in the loosely termed Wabanaki confederacy
to protect their territory.9 The Wabanaki recognized the European desire for exclu-
sive ownership over what, in Indigenous communities, were communal things:
animals and land. The Wabanaki learned fairly quickly that Europeans considered
animals to be property, and they experienced the impact of animal husbandry on
their traditional hunting territories as fences were erected and land was put to
pasture.10 Yet the Wabanaki did have a system of usufruct ownership, with dis-
tinctions between the lands belonging to one group from those of another. For
example, an Indigenous hunter could pursue game beyond his own land if he
was starving, if the hunt began on his lands, or if the animal was killed by mere
chance.11 There were rules in place to shape land use and to make amends when
required, a necessary function among such mobile people.
   In contrast to these complex and connected territorial histories, the imperial fic-
tions of Acadia and Nova Scotia were more closely connected to the whims of im-
perial administrators than to the lands over which they laid claim. The Wabanaki
had experience with Europeans by the time the French settled at Port Royal in
1604. English, French, and Spanish fishing boats had begun visiting northeastern
North America at least one hundred years earlier and, as a result, there had been
some cultural interaction, such as the development of a pidgin trade language.12
In 1603, Henry IV of France commissioned Pierre Du Gua, Sieur de Monts, a
Protestant nobleman, to establish a French settlement in North America. De Monts
was charged with converting the region’s Indigenous peoples and with prevent-
ing the English from encroaching on French territory. By 1608 France was focused
on Quebec, but Acadia continued to develop, in part because of the good relations
the French had established with the local Mi’kmaq and their leader, Membertou.13
Acadian settlements spread in the seventeenth century despite receiving scant at-
tention from the government in France; Louis XIV was kept busy consolidating his
authority, dealing with the Jansenists, and faring poorly in European military con-
flicts, which left little time to administer French settlements in North America.14
The Acadian settlements faced challenges from the Massachusetts Bay colony and
from the attempted Scottish settlement under Sir William Alexander in the 1620s.
The Acadians remained adaptable to geopolitical changes and kept as their focus
the survival and growth of their villages.15
   During the seventeenth century, the English had plantations between South
Carolina and Newfoundland, most of which were threatened by a combination of
French, Spanish, and Indigenous settlements. In the northeast, the English claimed
Nova Scotia by the 1621 grant of James VI of Scotland (James I of England) to
Sir William Alexander. Alexander, and later his son, attempted to settle in the re-
gion in the late 1620s and early 1630s but quickly quit the country in the face of
                                    Introduction                                  7


French and Indigenous resistance and inclement weather.16 The English Civil War,
the Restoration, and the Glorious Revolution kept much of the English govern-
ment’s focus on the European sphere, but concerns about overseas developments
remained on the administrative periphery.17

                                     •••
As European settlers arrived and hoped to expand, the Indigenous ability to de-
fine their homelands became an important element of protecting them. Europeans
did not introduce the concept of cartography to the Indigenous peoples of North
America. However, the Wabanaki understood geographic space and had rendered
it visually long before contact. Their graphic depictions were different from those
produced by Europeans, resulting in confusion (usually accidental, though per-
haps at times deliberate) when Europeans viewed Indigenous maps through a
western mindset. Despite this difference, Indigenous peoples generally, and the
Mi’kmaq specifically, were praised for their mapping skills. In the late seventeenth
century, the Recollet missionary Chrestien Le Clercq noted the Mi’kmaw ability to
map territory: “They have much ingenuity in drawing upon bark a kind of map
which marks exactly all the rivers and streams of a country of which they wish
to make a representation. They mark all the places thereon exactly and so well
that they make use of them successfully, and an Indian who possesses one makes
long voyages without going astray.”18 Le Clercq continued to describe how well
the Mi’kmaq knew their region and with what ease they travelled great distances
over dense terrain. Of particular importance was the missionary’s discovery that
the Mi’kmaq measured travel in terms of time and effort (using a variable scale)
and not simply distance.19 Short and difficult passages, for example, would receive
much more attention (orally or graphically) than longer easy routes. Indigenous
maps were temporal as much as territorial documents, and Indigenous cartogra-
phy reflected the distinct ways in which they moved through their environment.20
   European explorers and traders in North America relied heavily on Indigenous
cartography and oral instructions to facilitate their travels and inform their maps.
Interaction in the northeast was different than in places such as Africa or South
America. Africans were able to trade with Europeans without sharing territory.
They traded slaves for textiles and other goods along the shores without inviting
European settlement, leaving Africa’s territory largely under the control of Afri-
cans.21 In South America, the Spanish incorporated Indigenous peoples into their
towns or forced them into separate Spanish-controlled settlements. The Spanish
were also able to infiltrate Indigenous social systems and attempted to control
them from within. Certain Indigenous groups lived on the edges of Spanish settle-
ments while others remained autonomous, but many who came into contact with
the Spanish were heavily influenced by the European presence.22
   In the northeast, early explorers relied heavily on Indigenous support and geo-
graphic expertise. Samuel de Champlain depended on his Indigenous guides
when voyaging in the northeast, as did John Smith during his travels in Virginia
and New England. In his famous 1612 map of Virginia, Smith credited local In-
digenous peoples for providing him with geographic information that he later
incorporated into the image.23 On the other hand, Europeans who assumed that
8                                 Homelands and Empires


their method of mapping was universal often found themselves hopelessly lost. In
their voyage across America, Lewis and Clark read an Indigenous map the same
way they read all other maps and quickly discovered that they were off course.24
Like Europeans, Eastern Algonkians (a general linguistic group that included the
Wabanaki) mapped their geographic surroundings and described physical space
within the milieu of a specific world view, and when these mapping techniques
were taken out of context they lost much of their meaning.
  European settlers did not immediately recognize Indigenous spatial ideas and
the devices they used to delineate boundaries. As Europeans expanded inland
they encountered Indigenous groups with their own notions of geography. There
was variation between different Indigenous peoples; those with more experience
dealing with Europeans had adapted better to French and British cartographic
standards. While Indigenous peoples in the northeast left few maps, it is possible
to investigate their ideas of land use in the late-seventeenth and early-eighteenth
centuries by examining what little evidence does exist, including European ac-
counts and land deeds. For example, Sebastien Rale recounted the story of an
Indigenous man who believed that the English had captured the missionary.
“Having come to my quarters, and not finding me,” Rale described,

    Or any of those who had camped on the seashore, and who was ignorant of my return
    to the Village, caused a new alarm. Having come to my quarters, and not finding me
    … he did not doubt that we had been carried away by a party of Englishmen; and, go-
    ing on his way in order to inform the people of his own neighborhood, he came to the
    shore of a river. There he stripped the bark from a tree on which he drew with charcoal
    the English surrounding me, and one of the number cutting off my head (This is the
    only writing of the Savages, and they understand each other by figures of that kind as
    well as we understand each other by our letters).25

Rale continued to describe how the man affixed the image to a pole and left it by
the river’s shore. Shortly after, a group of Indigenous peoples arrived in the area,
saw the note, and were immediately informed of the presumed fate of the Jesuit.26
This account may have been embellished, or even fabricated for the readers’ inter-
est, but the methods Rale described suggested the importance of graphic images
among Indigenous groups.
  A more direct source of shared territorial interactions includes land deeds, which
provide an excellent opportunity to analyse cultural perspectives of space and
how geographic knowledge was put to use.27 Deeds most often included graphic
and written descriptions, and because they were created after some sort of intercul-
tural negotiation these sources capture both Indigenous and European ideas about
geography and the misunderstanding that often defined these relationships.28 At
the signing of the Treaty of Lancaster in 1744, for example, an Iroquois delegate
pointed to the secretary writing up the minutes and complained of the “pen-and-
ink work” that had robbed Indigenous peoples of so much territory.29 What be-
comes apparent is that Indigenous peoples and Europeans emphasized different
cartographic elements when creating a map. Working from a predominantly oral
culture, Indigenous peoples relied heavily on instructive toponyms. A place was
named for its physical features, the people who lived in the region, and for its
                                     Introduction                                   9


geographic location in relation to other places. Consequently, the name was not
simply a signifier, but also a mnemonic device.30 Another important component of
a name was its ecological element, suggesting how the area was used and for what
purposes. The name did not reflect ownership in the European sense, but outlined
the usufructuary relationship between Indigenous peoples and their surround-
ings.31 Administrators favoured European forms of geographic knowledge over
Indigenous spatial concepts, but both were included on the deeds. The process of
deed-making was twofold: an oral negotiation followed by the creation of an of-
ficial deed, signed by representatives for both sides.32
   Examples of graphic maps created by Indigenous peoples, including that put
down in 1666 by Metacom, known to the English at Massachusetts Bay as King
Philip, illustrate the relationship between text and context. Recording history (or,
in this case, depicting geography) was a form of power over what is remembered
and how the story is told.33 Metacom deeded a tract of land for which he supplied
an English-style map. His words accompanying the deed were recorded as fol-
lows: “This may informe the honord Court that I Phillip ame willing to sell the
Land within this draught; but the Indians that are upon it may live upon it still but
the land that is waste may be sold and Wattachopp is of the same mind; I have set
downe all the pricipall names of the land wee are not willing should be sold.”34
This ability to understand English cartography and employ it to retain Indigenous
use of granted lands was initially beneficial for Indigenous groups, but as the
number of settlers increased in the late-seventeenth century, Indigenous title and
usufructuary rights were ignored altogether. Territorial sovereignty was depen-
dent on the ability to enforce boundaries militarily. Despite the ultimate outcome
of these cultural encounters, Indigenous peoples’ detailed understandings of their
homelands, and their willingness to defend them, presented English settlers with
a challenge to expansion.35
   Exploring how the Wabanaki lived on their lands helps explain the kinds of re-
lationships they developed among themselves and with European settlers. North-
eastern homelands were influenced by transhumance, which Peter Pope defines as
more than “nomadic drifting,” but reflecting “seasonal movements by rural folk, in
search of farm and other employments.”36 The Wabanaki were not “rural folk,” but
the concept is equally applicable, as they were able to retain a remarkable degree
of continuity in the seasonal migration patterns despite the arrival and influence of
Europeans.37 Taking the Mi’kmaq as an example illuminates the importance of these
movements. During the spring and summer, Indigenous family hunting groups
lived along the shorelines or on major river systems to facilitate fishing. They prac-
ticed limited agriculture during the warm months, but as the temperatures cooled
they moved inland and prepared to spend the autumn hunting and fishing. Winter
brought the establishment of base camps from which the men would go on extend-
ed hunting trips. In the spring, settlements would move back to the shoreline.38 This
transhumance affected both the location and the size of Indigenous settlements.
Summer population centres were larger than winter sites, “which suggests a de-
gree of semi-permanency.”39 The Mi’kmaq continued their seasonal migrations and
adapted as necessary to the presence of the British and Acadians, but as Stephen J.
Hornsby has illustrated in the case of British America, competition between sys-
tems of geographic organization could easily lead to conflict.40
10                            Homelands and Empires


   Understanding Indigenous land use illustrates how the Wabanaki shaped French
and British settlement. Indigenous territorial boundaries existed and were en-
forced, but the way Indigenous peoples divided the land has been the subject of
debate among historians, anthropologists, and ethnohistorians.41 Indigenous peo-
ples recognized exclusive rights to land, but that exclusivity was never perma-
nent because villages would migrate every few years and abandon what land they
had been using. “The difference between Indians and Europeans,” argues William
Cronon, “was not that one had property and the other had none; rather, it was
that they loved property differently.”42 In the homelands, Indigenous geographic
boundaries were more fluid and susceptible to established migratory patterns,
but the boundaries existed, were enforced, and shaped how each group related
to their geography and to the region’s other inhabitants. Unfortunately, there are
few extant maps that present an Indigenous view of their territory. Perhaps the
best supplement we have is Joseph Aubery’s 1715 map (figure 1.1). Joseph Aubery,
a Jesuit priest who lived among the Wulstukwiuk on the Wulstukw River, knew
first-hand how important waterways were to the Indigenous peoples in Acadia.
Aubery had been educated in Paris and then moved to Quebec. It was there that
he learned the Abenaki language before accepting a post at Meductic, an impor-
tant village on the Wulstukw River “strategically located at the end of the inland
riverine and lacustrine route from Passamaquoddy and Penobscot country.”43 He
travelled extensively in the region, presumably with Indigenous guides, and be-
came a vocal proponent of resolving Acadia’s limits.44 He sent French officials at
Versailles reports and maps that detailed his vision of those boundaries, but what
is most apparent from his map is the dominating influence of inland rivers. Aside
from place names, there is no topographical detail. The map depicts only the com-
plex network of rivers used for inland travel.45

                                     •••
Exploring how different groups defined and used their surroundings helps ex-
plain both peaceful and violent encounters. Employing “spaces of power” in early
North America has been a useful avenue for understanding the way places (and
actions in them) shaped relationships between and among groups. For example,
cartographic historians have demonstrated how maps and representations of space
could direct imperial thinking, cultural interactions, and the relationships between
and among officials and settlers; however, the organization of geographic space to
facilitate or limit interaction raises new questions and new avenues for historical
research beyond the influence of representations of territory.46 For example, Stephen
J. Hornsby has illustrated how separate spaces of power in British North America
(Atlantic staple region, continental staple region, and agricultural frontier) con-
tributed to the Seven Years’ War, the American Revolution, and the ultimate “frac-
turing” of British North America.47 Elizabeth Mancke has refined the influence of
spaces of power by limiting her exploration to the early modern northeast. Mancke
argues that spaces of power are useful because they do not presuppose colonies,
they allow for overlapping centres with or without peripheries, and they provide
for a variety of economic, cultural, and imperial interactions.48 Yet spaces of power
were both geographic and temporal, and thus it is imperative to explore the
1.1 Jesuit missionary Joseph Aubery’s map of Acadia and New France (1715), which illustrates the importance of inland waterways along the
           northeast coast. Dépôt des Cartes et Plans de la Service Hydrographique Archives, 124-1-4, Bibliothèque National, Paris.
12                             Homelands and Empires


construction, maintenance, and ultimate dissolution of specific sites of interac-
tion. It is important to offer a further refinement to this model by exploring how
geographic organization and spaces of power enabled peaceful relations or en-
gendered violent conflict within a given territory based on nearly constant nego-
tiations among its European and Indigenous inhabitants. These spaces were not
established or permanent spheres of influence, but rather temporary, fluid, and
seasonal places of interaction and interchange that could be constructed, main-
tained, and allowed to expire as necessary.
   Negotiation was key to most relationships among Indigenous peoples, imperial
officials, and settlers. As Jack P. Greene has argued, the generally peaceful years be-
tween 1713 and 1763 were shaped by demographic growth and general prosperity
in the British American colonies.49 Many studies that are focused on broad themes,
such as negotiation, lack specificity, however, and places such as Nova Scotia are
either ignored or subsumed into New England’s sphere.50 Examining the fictive
empires and established homelands in the northeast emphasizes the importance
of considering a specific geographic region on its own terms. Too often historians
analyse one place by its relationship to another – centre and periphery, frontier and
settlement, borderlands – in ways that elide the fact that a region’s inner workings
were often shaped by immediate spatial concerns rather than larger imperial pol-
icy.51 Empires were continually negotiated, but the centre-periphery model does
not fully express the complexity on the “periphery,” which was shaped by fluid
and often temporary spaces in which power and authority were negotiated on the
ground. Homelands were not borderlands, by which I mean regions of wilderness
that separated different groups and allowed for interactions that could not take
place elsewhere.52 Rather, Indigenous spaces into which European powers hoped
to expand were more complex. Yet local authorities in these regions sacrificed the
declarations of sovereignty emanating from imperial centres to enable settlers in
or near the pales to establish a presence, survive reprisals, and, hopefully, coexist.
Negotiations to this end took place not only in governors’ mansions and customs
houses, but also in the seasonal rhythms and temporary sites of exchange that
brought different groups together for the practical purpose of securing continued
interactions acceptable to all involved. The process by which British Nova Scotia
overpowered French Acadia and challenged Indigenous homelands was one of
many possible outcomes and can be investigated as a process of territorial negotia-
tion and competition.
   It is important to ask not just “where” but also “when” we might find the blurry
boundaries that separated imperial fictions from Indigenous homelands. Pursuing
these investigations across both time and space requires fully incorporating what
became Nova Scotia into the early American northeast, a task too rarely under-
taken by historians of early America. Because Nova Scotia failed to join the rebel-
ling American colonies in 1776, the province is often ignored in surveys of British
American colonies, save for a brief appearance as the site of the Acadian expulsion
during the Seven Years’ War.53 Daniel K. Richter’s recent survey, Before the Revolution,
offers a more even-handed and inclusive account of early North America that offers
a number of intellectual themes that help explain Nova Scotia’s position, even if
Richter himself does not often discuss the settlement.54 Canadian historians, influ-
enced by Ian McKay’s “liberal order” framework, have increasingly focused on the
                                     Introduction                                  13


post-1830 era while ignoring the roots of individual liberalism that are visible in an
earlier period.55 The increasing prominence of Atlantic history – a discipline meant
to challenge imperial and nationalist narratives by blurring cultural and political
boundaires – provided new opportunities to integrate Nova Scotia more fully into
the northern Atlantic. While recent work is making strides towards that goal, the
field has generally ignored Acadia in favour of New England and, more recently,
the Caribbean.56 It is encouraging, however, that the growing influence of Loyalist
studies has bolstered Nova Scotia’s prominence as historians look beyond the
American Revolution to those who remained loyal to the Crown, many of whom
made their way to Nova Scotia and, after 1784, New Brunswick.57
   Indigenous and imperial competition in the northeast foreshadowed the British
response to the fall of New France, a period when a large portion of North America
was integrated into the British world through various cartographic and visual de-
pictions that helped make new territorial acquisitions appear familiar to a British
audience.58 Maps and boundaries were instrumental in British America after 1763,
as evidenced by the conflict engendered by the enlargement of Quebec after 1774
and the growing sense of an American identity that defined itself by westward
expansion.59 If we accept Ramsay Cook’s conclusion that the conquest of Quebec
and the incorporation of French Canadians – primarily Catholic – into a largely
Protestant Canada was the defining feature of Canadian history, we can perhaps
extend that argument more broadly to early North America.60 The competition
between British Nova Scotia and French Acadia after 1690 was not simply an early
example of British forces attempting to assert authority over French Catholics.
Rather, this example illustrates the methods by which European and Indigenous
powers in North America competed, negotiated, and used concepts of land and
territory over long periods of time to address the political, economic, and social
challenges that attended increased interaction. The concept of territorial conquest
has outlived its utility; John G. Reid et al. recently questioned the meaning of the
Acadian “conquest” of 1710, arguing that there was as much continuity as change
in the years that followed the British capture of Port Royal.61 In place of simplistic
notions of conquest, we must, as Lauren Benton has demonstrated, investigate the
processes by which competing groups addressed local contexts and contingen-
cies that shaped both violent conflict and peaceful coexistence.62 That is the task I
have set for myself in this book. Competing notions of territory and environment,
manifested in European ideas of empire and Indigenous protection of homelands,
shaped the kinds of military, economic, and cultural interactions that took place in
the Northeast. It would be impossible to measure and evaluate every aspect of this
process. At times, especially when the topic turns to imperial competition between
Britain and France, homelands will fade to the background. They fall out of the
discussion not because Indigenous territories were suddenly less consequential in
absolute terms, but rather because they did not shape imperial discussions taking
place in the offices of administrative officials. In addition, though we know that
eventually the British defeated the French and continued their slow expansion into
Mi’kma’ki, Wulstukwik, and the Dawnland, we must not read that outcome back-
wards. In 1690, when a ragtag group of English soldiers captured Port Royal from
the French, few residents in the northeast – Indigenous, English, or French – could
have predicted what the region would look like a century later.
14                             Homelands and Empires


                                      •••
The chapters that follow are arranged chronologically. Chapter 1 explores the ten-
sions between French and English imperial fictions as pales struggling to establish
a sense of permanence within Mi’kma’ki, Wulstukwik, and the Dawnland from
1690 to 1710. Chapter 2 investigates four major events that shaped how imagined
empires interacted with Indigenous homelands from the “conquest” of Port Royal
in 1710 to the British-Wabanaki treaties of 1725–6: the Treaty of Utrecht, Mi’kmaw
and Wabanaki expressions of territorial sovereignty, French-British boundary
negotiations, and British-Indigenous treaties. Chapter 3 argues that 1726 to 1744
was a period of relative peace in the northeast governed locally by a set of shared
spaces – economic, diplomatic, and religious – that provided seasonal, temporary,
and fleeting areas of interaction. The years leading up to the founding of Halifax
serve as the central topic of chapter 4, signalling as it did a change in British impe-
rial policy between 1744 and 1755 that warranted adjustments from the Wabanaki
and the French, who became increasingly aware of British officials’ intentions
to expand in the northeast. In chapter 5 we telescope away from the northeast
and focus on the Acadian boundary debates that took place in Paris from 1750 to
1755, during which imperial officials pored over maps and reports to argue for the
prominence of their particular geographic fiction. Chapter 6 takes a more broadly
Atlantic approach to the years from 1755 to 1763: sifting through depictions and
debates about the northeast as they appeared in popular magazines; examining
violent encounters and the Acadian deportation; and exploring resettlement and
Indigenous treaties resulting from the Seven Years’ War. By 1763, Acadia had faded
away and Nova Scotia had connected its pales, expanded into an administrative
unit, and transitioned from an imperial fiction to a British plantation that posed a
significant and growing threat to the northeast homelands.

                      Dates, Translations, and Terminology

Britain used the Julian (Old Style (O.S.)) calendar until 1752. This system marked
the new year on March 25th, and was eleven days behind the Gregorian (New
Style) calendar in force in France, where the new year began on January 1st. To
avoid confusion, this book has simply followed the dates as they were noted at the
time. When a record is used to describe an event in Britain and France, both Old-
and New-style dates are included. After 1752, there is no discrepancy between
British and French dates.
   All translations from the French primary sources, published records, and sec-
ondary literature, unless otherwise noted, are mine.
   The names of peoples, places, and empires can be somewhat confounding, but
I have attempted to remain consistent throughout. Thus, England and English are
used before 1707, at which point the Act of Union created Britain and Britons.
Indigenous terms and toponyms are used when referring to Indigenous peoples
and places. These names are renditions in English of originals in another language.
I have focused on being unambiguous, but am well aware that spellings have
changed (and will continue to do so) over time.
               1 Neighbours in the Homeland




Not much changed after 10 May 1690 when the English attacked Port Royal, a
small fort tucked away from the Bay of Fundy, separated from the larger body of
water by a narrow opening leading into a river that wound its way roughly paral-
lel to the Fundy shore. Port Royal in the summer of 1690 was not too different from
what it had been in the fall, save for the fact that the English now claimed control
over the fort and its surroundings. The surrender on 10 May had not been pretty.
Without so much as firing a cannon, Louis-Alexandre des Friches de Menneval,
the governor of Acadia, had relinquished the fort under favourable terms. The
English took control of the small Vauban-style fort set on the Rivière au Dauphin.
The fort was set on a small hill fronted by marshlands. Behind Port Royal could
be found a small community consisting of a few houses, fields, and a hospital sur-
rounded by more marshland, some of which had been dyked in the Acadian style.
By use of an ingenious aboiteau, a dyking system that combined an earthen levee,
sluices, and a clapper valve, the Acadians drained the saltwater and protected the
fertile land from the Bay of Fundy’s tides.1 However, by 12 May, after Sir William
Phips (who had led the New England troops in their attack on Acadia) discov-
ered just how weak Port Royal was, he had the churches smashed, looted the fort,
and seized Menneval, in direct contradiction to the terms of capitulation. Phips
pressed the French residents to swear an oath to King William and Queen Mary,
appointed a French sergeant to command the fort, named six leading Acadians as
a council, and made preparations to leave. On their return to Boston, the English
sailed around the southern tip of the peninsula to burn houses at LaHave, a fish-
ing village (and former capital of Acadia) on Acadia’s Atlantic coast. Apparently
Phips’s expedition was all that was required to “conquer” Acadia, a swathe of land
stretching from the Gaspé to the Kennebec. In 1691, Colonel Edward Tyng was
named governor (though he never took up residence in the “colony”) and the 1691
charter of Massachusetts from William and Mary incorporated Acadia into that
colony (which was met with much indifference from the Acadians).2
   In 1690, the French quickly surrendered Port Royal. The English consistently
ignored the fort and Acadia generally, which remained inhabited by the Mi’kmaq,
Wulstukwiuk, Passamaquoddy, and Acadians. The region might have been called
“Acadia” by the French, or even “Nova Scotia” by the English, but even after 1690
this part of the world was Mi’kma’ki, Wulstukwik, and the Dawnland, homelands
of the Mi’kmaq, Wulstukwiuk, Passamaquoddy, and their allies in the loosely or-
ganized Wabanaki confederacy.3 To examine northeastern North America in the
16                            Homelands and Empires


late-seventeenth or early-eighteenth centuries as a collection of European colonies
is anachronistic. What would eventually become Nova Scotia, New Brunswick,
and Maine was under the control of Indigenous groups during this period. The
Mi’kmaq, Wulstukwiuk, and Passamaquoddy coexisted with some settlers, in-
cluding French Acadians who lived and farmed along the Fundy coast. These
relationships were not without tension and some violence, but Port Royal was
spared the death and devastation that visited settlements such as Jamestown.
Other European groups, especially English administrators and military personnel,
found themselves living in a handful of pales protected by forts. Administrators
in London and Versailles were aware of the region’s potential, especially its fish-
ery, but affairs in Europe prevented any sustained efforts to assert sovereignty in
northeastern North America.4
   Acadia and Nova Scotia were imperial fictions in the northeast that European
administrators hoped could be transformed into functioning administrative units
that controlled territory. As the French in Paris and the English in London strug-
gled to transform their ideas of empire into a reality, settlers on the ground in
these Indigenous homelands created a matrix of cultural, commercial, and politi-
cal alliances with each other and the region’s Indigenous peoples. Consequently,
Mi’kma’ki and Wulstukwik became complicated by a set of geographic relation-
ships and competing spatial identities with limits that extended and retracted based
on Indigenous-European alliances. Determining the region’s geopolitical boundar-
ies (a near obsession among British and French officials) was impossible because of
confusion and competition among and within the English, French, and Indigenous
groups that lived there. Imperial distractions in Europe prevented the development
of policy or diplomacy that could peacefully resolve the issues between Britain
and France, and the strength of Wabanaki warriors and diplomats on the ground,
coupled with the material weakness of local administrators meant that boundar-
ies could not be established through military force. A general paucity of maps,
surveys, or reports prevented English and French officials from drawing lines to
separate their putative territories and Indigenous allies. French officials in Acadia
benefitted from their alliance with the Mi’kmaq and Wulstukwiuk, who launched
attacks when English settlements pushed into Wulstukwik and Mi’kma’ki. As far
as French officials were concerned, these Indigenous allies acted as a fence keeping
the English out of Acadia. The Mi’kmaq, Wulstukwiuk, and Passamaquoddy, for
their part, were doing nothing more than protecting their homeland. The English
lacked a firm alliance with northeastern Indigenous peoples – though at times they
made overtures to win favour among the Abenaki – and thus were limited in their
ability to extend New England northwards as desired by officials in Boston.5 Before
1710 (and, indeed, well after), Nova Scotia and Acadia were nothing more than
places that existed on a few maps and in the minds of many officials, but not on
the ground.
   For European settlement in the northeast, 1690 was a low point.6 Acadia had
been captured by Phips, but ignored; Maine, whose settlers had worked to carve
out their independence from Massachusetts, had been destroyed by Indigenous
raids; and in both New England and New France, local administrators had a
difficult time convincing their superiors to consider – much less support – sus-
tained development or military engagements. Louis XIV of France and William
                             Neighbours in the Homeland                             17


III of England were otherwise occupied. Both were embroiled in the Nine Years’
War (known as King William’s War in North America, a campaign of which in-
cluded the capture of Port Royal in 1690), while William III had the additional
task of securing his claim to the English throne in the face of opposition from
the Catholic James II after the Glorious Revolution of 1688.7 Both England and
France were suffering depleted treasuries and weakened armies, and both hoped
to rebuild and reorganize their “fiscal-military state” at the earliest opportunity.8
Consequently, daily life and political structures in the northeast retained a signifi-
cantly local character that responded to Atlantic developments beyond the control
of American administrators.
   While the Indigenous peoples of the northeast had boundaries based on inti-
mate knowledge of woodlands, river ways, and a long history of interaction with
each other, such was not the case for European forces. From an imperial perspec-
tive, a deep understanding of territory and internal boundaries was both signifi-
cant and elusive in this era as certain British and French settlements – especially
New York and Canada – came into sharper focus, while others – namely Acadia
and northern New England – suffered from competing claims and expectations
that defied English and French capabilities in those regions. After the restoration
of Charles II in 1660, there was a territorial reorganization along the Atlantic coast:
New Netherland became New York, Connecticut received specific borders, and
grants were given for East and West Jersey, Pennsylvania, Delaware, and Carolina.
French traders were also beginning to migrate south along the Mississippi River
into regions previously unoccupied by Europeans. The northeast, however, re-
mained a region of overlapping and competing territorial claims that couldn’t be
resolved by simple diplomacy or an exchange of maps. In Newfoundland, for ex-
ample, French and English interests clashed over territory and fishing rights even
though the island’s geography remained largely speculative until well into the
eighteenth century. D’Iberville went so far as to expel hundreds of English settlers
from the Avalon Peninsula in 1696–97, an episode akin to the fate of the Acadians
two generations later. One major difference, however, was that Newfoundland’s
valuable fishery had ensured the island was on the figurative map for England
and France much earlier than Acadia, so there was a maritime and military pres-
ence on the coasts that worked to govern the island and assert claims.9 Thus, if
Newfoundland fell within a more thoroughly English Atlantic space, and the new
English colonies were more bordered and defined, Nova Scotia and Acadia were
anticipated settlements in a region claimed by both crowns but controlled by the
Indigenous population. Both European powers struggled to overcome the com-
petition they faced from the rival crown and, especially, to win over or defeat the
Indigenous inhabitants of Mi’kma’ki, Wulstukwik, and the Dawnland. As New
France expanded into the interior and English colonies sprang up along the coast,
the northeast became increasingly significant as a potential settlement, but frus-
tratingly elusive as a defined province with accepted boundaries.
   The period between the “conquests” in French Acadia – the English capture
of Port Royal in 1690 (returned to France at the Treaty of Ryswick in 1697), and
the later British capture of that same fort in 1710 – was an era of almost constant
restructuring and rebuilding. Like their counterparts in Europe, administrators
in New England and New France worked to redraw boundaries, launch attacks,
18                             Homelands and Empires


and develop effective strategies with Indigenous peoples. In reality, these practices
were mutually dependent as only Indigenous groups could enforce regional divi-
sions. It is central to explore three factors that combined to limit imperial influence
in Indigenous homelands in the northeast: first, developments in Europe distract-
ed monarchs and officials from what was considered a peripheral part of North
America; second, local administrative confusion over boundaries and internal
competition among settlers and officials prevented either New England or New
France from making defensible claims to territory beyond their forts and limited
local settlements; finally, and most important, the reliance on Indigenous groups
– who suffered their own internal divisions and conflicts – to establish and patrol
imperial divisions was an implicit recognition of Indigenous sovereignty. As a re-
sult, the period from 1690 to 1710 was one of fluid settlements, elusive claims to
authority, and only projected European power in the northeastern homelands.

                                       •••
It must have been a very frustrating time for Louis de Buade de Frontenac et de
Palluau, the governor general of New France. First appointed as governor in 1672,
Frontenac had from the earliest days battled with Quebec’s sovereign council, the
Intendant, the clergy, and, of course, the Iroquois. His inability to respect the limits
to his authority – as evidenced by his imprisonment of the Intendant’s teenage son
and a senior member of the Sovereign council for supposed acts of disrespect to
his person – had Frontenac recalled in 1682.10 At the outbreak of the Nine Years’
War (1688–97), Frontenac was reappointed governor general of New France and
charged with leading a French attack on New York. Quebec had suffered attacks
by the Iroquois, and French officials hoped that Frontenac could both secure
the settlement from future Indigenous campaigns (supported as they were by the
English at New York) and weaken the English by attacking their settlements in the
northeast. Frontenac was delayed leaving France, and by the time he arrived in
Quebec it was too late to attack New York. The plans were scuttled and Frontenac
was forced to regroup.11
   In late 1690, Frontenac successfully defended Quebec against an English at-
tack led from Boston. England’s capture of Port Royal that May had encouraged
Massachusetts officials to launch an attempt on the French capital in August.12
But Frontenac’s hopes of taking the battle to New York and New England were
thwarted by European distractions. “I am aware that in the present state of affairs,”
he lamented in 1691 to the minister of the Marine, Louis Phélypeaux, comte de
Pontchartrain, “it will perhaps be difficult to think of matters so remote.”13 But for
Frontenac, this mission was of singular importance. He wanted to attack Boston to
see if it would surrender. Even if it did not, the attack would occupy the settlement
and prevent New England reinforcements from coming to the aid of Manhattan,
“the capture whereof is alone necessary for the security of this country.”14
   A variety of similar arguments for attacking English settlements were equally
unsuccessful. Defending Canada – which, admittedly, was not the same as attack-
ing New England – would secure the fur trade, provide access to timber for the
French navy, offer a market for French wines and brandy, and, perhaps most im-
portant, preserve the glory and piety of France. A 1691 memoir on Canada stated
                            Neighbours in the Homeland                            19


that supporting the settlement “comports with his Majesty’s glory not to abandon
over ten thousand of his subjects, in this country, and who would there be put
to the sword or constrained to submit to the Conqueror’s yoke.”15 If la gloire and
the Catholic faith were insufficient reasons for defending New France, Frontenac
was prepared to offer another: chasing the English from the coasts of Acadia and
Newfoundland would grant France access to commerce in fish valued at 20 mil-
lion livres, money that could be spent in the conquest of “les Indes.”16 But even
calls for strengthening the glory of the king, supporting the Catholic faith, and
securing funds for France’s imperial endeavours elsewhere failed to win the sup-
port of Louis XIV’s administrators for an attack on the English.
   One significant concern was the government’s ignorance of New France’s ge-
ography. Acadia was an amorphous region without a real sense of permanence.17
The boundary between New France and New England was murky, and the divi-
sions within New France itself – where did Canada end and Acadia begin? – were
also largely unknown. Officials in Quebec admitted that Canada was susceptible
to attacks from the Iroquois, who knew the forests and serpentine waterways so
well.18 Lack of this ability to traverse the region with ease, when coupled with the
king’s preoccupations in France were French officials’ biggest barrier to launch-
ing attacks on the English. The king would have organized a force to attack New
England and New York, Pontchartrain informed Frontenac, “if the present state of
his affairs had permitted it and that, when feasible, he will not let the opportunity
pass,” but he needed a better idea of the local geography – including maps and
soundings – before any additional measures could be taken.19 It became impera-
tive for local officials to share what they knew about their surroundings to ensure
that administrators in France could understand why attacks were necessary and
how they might be carried out.
   It fell to Joseph Robinau de Villebon – the controversial commandant of Acadia
whose numerous detractors charged that he was too focused on using his posi-
tion for personal gain rather than securing and governing Acadia – to provide
a more detailed account of the geography of French territories in the northeast.
Villebon replaced Menneval after William Phips took the latter prisoner follow-
ing the surrender of Port Royal in 1690. Villebon had served under Menneval, but
had been recalled to France and so was absent during the attack on Port Royal.20
Upon his return to Acadia, Villebon relocated the Acadian headquarters to the
Saint John River, staying first at Jemseg (a site previously occupied by an English
fort) and then constructing new defences farther up the Saint John at the mouth
of the Nashwaak River (Fredericton, NB). Moving headquarters posed a direct
challenge to the English claims to Acadia. Had Phips conquered the fort only or
the entirety of French Acadia? Massachusetts’s new Charter of 1691 incorporated
Acadia into the Massachusetts government, but with no English presence in the re-
gion and the re-establishment of a French government on the Saint John River, the
English did little more than trace new colonial borders that had no meaning on the
ground.21 Villebon’s instructions were to encourage France’s Indigenous allies –
the Wabanaki – to harass the English at every turn.22 He also sent whatever reports,
surveys, or cartographic sketches he could muster to France. In early November
1692, Villebon attached a list of English forts (and the distances between them)
stretching from Pemaquid to Boston. Two weeks later, he sent a letter detailing
20                             Homelands and Empires


the return to Acadia of a Huguenot from Bordeaux who had spent time in Boston.
According to Villebon, this man knew the coasts very well, had captured eleven
enemy ships, and was willing to aid the French cause for a small recompense.23
This type of practical knowledge of the inlets, harbours, and bays along the coast
was useful in no small part because it could be used to create exact maps. By the
summer of 1693, with rumours swirling that Boston was preparing an attack on
New France, the administration called for this combination of vernacular knowl-
edge with cartographic expertise “pour en dresser les cartes et verifier celles qui on
esté faictes.”24 Wary of a tactical misstep in North America, French officials wanted
a well-crafted plan before launching an attack.
  The English attack never came. Rumours of a renewed campaign from Boston
masked the fact that the English were rarely interested in true “conquest.” Despite
French concerns and preparations for both offensive and defensive measures, the
English remained indifferent towards Acadia and failed to launch an attack on
New France until 1711. A charter from the British monarch in 1691 incorporated
“the territory Called Accada or Nova Scotia” into the Province of Massachusetts
Bay, but after Tyng was captured during his brief visit to the province, the English
didn’t bother to replace him.25 There was an English pattern developing that
favoured peaceful negotiation and elite co-option over sustained military pres-
ence. Phips’s attitude towards Port Royal recalled the English capture of New
Netherland in 1664 and foreshadowed not only the 1710 conquest of Port Royal
but also the 1759 capture of Quebec.26 The effort required to capture and hold a fort
or region during war paled in comparison to the negotiations necessary to assert
authority during peace.
  Even as the threat of an English attack failed to materialize, officials in Acadia,
Quebec, and France mounted preparations, fretted over defensive measures, and
worked to secure Indigenous support. Villebon received word in early 1694 that
the King had outfitted a ship, the Bretonne, commanded by Simon-Pierre Denys
de Bonaventure, to transport supplies to Acadia to bolster defences at Nashwaak
and help wage war on the English. French officials were concerned that their
Indigenous allies, particularly the Abenaki, had been forced to trade with the
English to secure the goods they needed.27 The Bretonne was carrying gifts for the
French allies, and Villebon was instructed to give the best presents to the Abenaki
to make up for previous shortages.28 Villebon himself was likely responsible for
the misallocation of gifts, as he had been accused by a number of officials and
inhabitants of embezzling trade goods, defrauding troops of their supplies, and
unduly profiting from the fur trade.29 The want of presents had led the Abenaki
to sign an agreement with the English in 1693, which left the French in Acadia
susceptible to English attack. However, Abenaki factionalism – specifically the rise
of a pro-French group in 1694 – prevented the English from capitalizing on the
momentary alliance.30
  Satisfying Indigenous allies was an important part of the French military strat-
egy, and Pontchartrain encouraged Frontenac to support Wabanki attacks on the
English whenever possible. “Were it not for [these attacks on New England], the
[English] would be able to employ all their forces in a more vigorous defence
against your expeditions,” Pontchartrain warned, “and perhaps to attack you in
the centre, or at the extremities at the Upper part of the Colony.”31 French officials
                             Neighbours in the Homeland                            21


were aware that the Abenaki were concerned about the English fort at Pemaquid,
one of the northernmost English settlements (present-day Bristol, Maine) that had
previously fallen to Abenaki forces in 1689, and so they promised to attack the fort
the following year.32 Attacks were launched – a combined effort of French forc-
es, the Abenaki, and Jean-Vincent d’Abbadie, Baron de Saint-Castin, who was a
French officer and Abenaki chief – which captured Pemaquid and destroyed small
settlements along the coast, leading to plans for future attacks towards Boston.33
These attacks terrified the English. They grew to fear the winter most of all, when
Abenaki warriors launched surprise attacks on snowshoes and escaped quickly
into the forests before the English could respond.34
   The final years of the Nine Years’ War witnessed more preparations than actual
attacks, and the possibility of peace brought increased attention to the region’s
competing boundaries. Officials in France, more concerned with the European the-
atre, offered assurances that Acadia was important but focused on increasing the
number of Indigenous attacks on New England in lieu of French campaigns. In
England, the Committee of Privy Council for Trade and Foreign Plantations (the
administrative body commonly called the Board of Trade, which was responsible
to the secretary of state for the Southern Department and charged with overseeing
England’s colonial affairs) took a more active interest in the American northeast.35
In October 1696, the Board of Trade responded to a request from the Admiralty for
a report on naval stores in New England by suggesting a survey of the entire coast
from Massachusetts to Nova Scotia to collect information on the nature of the land,
extant forts, and suitable sites for new settlements.36 The French were also circulat-
ing maps of the northeast. Jean-Baptiste-Louis Franquelin, the king’s mapmaker at
Quebec, visited officials in Paris carrying his drafts and maps of the Acadian coast
and English forts.37 These meetings were meant to help administrators formulate a
plan of attack, but the information itself would become equally useful during and
after the Treaty of Ryswick as British and French officials argued over the extent of
Acadia and the location of important Indigenous allies.
   The treaty signalled concern and confusion over boundaries in North America,
specifically the limits of Acadia. France had fought well in Europe, but during
the war there had been a noticeable shift in Louis XIV’s military policy from of-
fensive engagements to a more defensive strategy.38 Even after signing the treaty
on 20 September 1697, French officials compiled a list of topics to be settled with
England resulting from the Ryswick agreement. The first item on the list was
“regler les limites de l’acadie en execution du traité de Breda.”39 The Treaty of
Breda, signed in 1667, ended the Anglo-French conflict of 1666–67 and the second
Anglo-Dutch war (1665–67); it also ended putative English possession of French
Acadia in Mi’kma’ki, which had been under the governorship of Sir Thomas Tem-
ple since 1656. Exactly what had been claimed and what was being returned re-
mained unclear, and competing claims to Temple’s land would busy New England
officials well into the 1690s.40 French delegates to the Ryswick discussions argued
that ­Temple had controlled – and thus surrendered at Breda – the region stretch-
ing from the peninsular isthmus to Pentagouët on the Penobscot River (Castine,
Maine), an old French post often considered the southern limit of Acadia.41 Breda’s
putative Acadian boundaries thus appealed to French officials anxious to secure as
much coastal territory as possible.
22                             Homelands and Empires


   At this juncture, Indigenous territorial divisions played a role in European land
claims, though without the involvement of Indigenous inhabitants. Ultimately no
decisions were made about Acadia’s boundaries at Ryswick, but it was agreed that
commissioners would be appointed to determine the region’s extent. So English
and French officials on both sides of the Atlantic began marshalling evidence to
support their specific claims. Though neither European power could exert much
control over the region, which remained under Mi’kmaw, Wulstukwiuk, and
Passa­maquoddy control, both English and French officials emphasized the impor-
tance of Indigenous boundaries that could be assigned to European powers. John
Nelson, a Boston merchant with trade ties to Acadia, offered his thoughts on the
negotiations. Nelson knew Acadia and Quebec well. He had been on good terms
with the French government and held a trade warehouse at Port Royal. He was
captured, along with Colonel Tyng, in 1691 and kept at Quebec. When his ploy to
smuggle a note to Boston warning of a coming raid was foiled, Nelson was sent
to prison in France and only released in 1694.42 In 1697, Nelson warned the Board
of Trade not to allow the French to bar English fishermen from the Acadian coast.
Of equal importance was protecting both trade with the Indigenous peoples and
the boundaries around New York. The French, Nelson argued, used their alliance
with Indigenous groups to encroach on New York by taking advantage of the riv-
ers running behind the colony. According to the merchant, both nations should be
free to travel and traffic through Indigenous homelands.43
   The following month Nelson wrote an additional opinion at the request of the
Board of Trade in which he focused on Acadia’s boundaries and how they related
to Indigenous alliances. Nelson, who was in Paris at the time, had managed to get
a sense of the French idea of Acadia. He had learned that the French would sug-
gest the Kennebec as the boundary between Acadia and New England. This argu-
ment would be made under the “plausible pretence” that the Kennebec was the
largest river flowing from the coast through much of the northeast, almost to the
St Lawrence. As such a “noted boundary,” the French would be able to hold their
Indigenous allies from crossing over to the western side, which would be accepted
as English territory.44 Nelson did not buy the argument; in fact, he argued that
the Indigenous peoples would be so angered at having their lands granted to the
French – a tacit sign of English territorial weakness – that they would increase their
attacks on New England. Much better, he thought, to set the boundary at St George
River, five leagues (almost 28 kilometres) east of Pemaquid (which was the colo-
nial boundary in the time of Sir Thomas Temple).45 It was better to work at rebuild-
ing the English-Abenaki relationship than to rely on the French to control their
Indigenous allies. However, Nelson didn’t fully understand the nature of Eastern
Abenaki boundaries in the Dawnland and he overestimated French influence on
its residents. The Kennebec River, including a Jesuit mission site at Norridgewock
established by Sebastien Rale, cut through Kennebec territory. The Kennebec and
their neighbours, the Pequawket and Arosaguntacook to the west and Penobscot to
the east, divided their lands by the highlands between river basins. Consequently,
the territory of one group “simply faded away” as it approached that of another.46
European settlers and officials had a difficult time understanding this kind of ter-
ritorial division, which didn’t lend itself to firm colonial boundaries.
   Nelson’s arguments reflected his priorities as a merchant. Should the French
boundary be accepted, the English would lose “four or five” of their best fishing
                             Neighbours in the Homeland                            23


islands and harbours, not to mention the forests of spruce, fir, birch, sugar maple,
and pine that could serve for masts, timber, and deal-boards (plank wood). The land
itself was fit for settlement, an idea Nelson supported as it would bring the Abenaki
into contact with the English under peaceful terms and allow new trade relations to
blossom into alliances. It was not a natural hatred that made the Abenaki enemies,
he continued, but rather the mismanagement of trade that the French exploited
to turn the Indigenous peoples against the English. Because the Abenaki were ul-
timately self-interested, they would in time be won over by the favourable trade
relations the English provided.47 Nelson made no mention of other elements of al-
liances. By disregarding the cultural and religious connections linking the French
and their Indigenous allies, he provided English officials with a political calculus
that ignored a central component of alliance making in the northeast.48
   While the Board of Trade considered Nelson’s proposals, the French king and
his ministers worked to consolidate their power over the geographic fiction of
Acadia. Worried that too many seigneurial grantees in Acadia had failed to meet
their settlement obligations – such as clearing land and making it productive – and
concerned that some had engaged in illegal trade with England, officials in France
demanded that all landholders remit their titles for inspection.49 Administrators
also offered their own proposals on how best to separate Acadia from New Eng­
land. One option was to leave the Kennebec River free for navigation by both na-
tions, and prohibit the construction of buildings or settlements for five leagues on
either side.50 Limiting the introduction of new pales would have created a buffer
between the English and the French, while preventing New England settlers from
venturing into Abenaki territory around the Kennebec. The English were unlikely
to accept such a proposition given their desire to settle among the Abenaki up to
the St George River and thereby win their friendship, as John Nelson had sug-
gested. The Abenaki, on the other hand, stood to benefit from this arrangement.
Unlike other Indigenous groups who “became landless mercenaries” of either
England or France, the Abenaki “perceived that their survival depended on hold-
ing the middle ground,” and thus they “remained an important third force be-
tween New England and New France.”51 An Indigenous buffer zone would allow
the Abenaki to maintain their religious ties with the French and their economic re-
lationship with the English, both of which served Abenaki interests.52 Recognizing
the Kennebec watershed as the Abenaki homeland, and relying on these groups
to prevent European encroachment, was a practical solution to preventing conflict
between European powers that had created imperial fictions without fully under-
standing the environment or possessing the military strength to defend or expand
pales in the Dawnland.
   Officials in France and Acadia valued the coastland connecting Mi’kma’ki,
Wulstukwik, and the Dawnland, which consisted of potential fishery sites, marsh-
lands, and forests, and thus hoped to retain as much of it as possible. The English
intercepted at least one French letter outlining the region’s potential, raising con-
cerns about French intensions to develop the Atlantic coast and reap profits from
relatively meagre investments. The letter, addressed to the king, described the
value of the territory running from the entrance of the St Lawrence to the English
dominions. By investing a quarter of what France had spent on Quebec, this region
would be brought into condition to produce more in one year than Quebec would
produce in forty. The coast was suited for wine, salt, and corn, and its forests could
24                             Homelands and Empires


furnish wood for shipbuilding. Trade in salt fish, herrings, mackerel, and salmon
