would relieve France of its dependence on England and Holland. Once settled, the
letter concluded, this region could produce a profit of two million livres a year,
while also serving as a nursery for seamen in the French fleet.53 A memorial writ-
ten the following year averred that France could claim not just the coast to New
England, but the entire stretch of land from Newfoundland to Florida. The French
discovered that territory and would have taken possession of it before the English,
“mais les Anglois,” the memoir continued, “profitant du malheur de nos guerres
domestiques, qui occuperent les arms de nos Roy Francois” from Charles IX to
Louis XIII, established their colonies in regions discovered by order of the French
king.54 The king’s obligations in Europe cost France territory in North America.
   The debate over Acadia as an imperial fiction – and who could rightfully claim
the territory – persisted in the years leading up to the War of the Spanish Succession.
While French officials claimed that France had discovered most of North America’s
Atlantic coast, the Board of Trade credited that feat to English subjects. In a letter
to Secretary of State James Vernon, the Board of Trade outlined the putative his-
tory of English possession in the northeast from King James’s patent to the Council
of Plymouth in 1620 to the annexation of Nova Scotia by charter to the Colony
of Massachusetts Bay in 1691.55 English fishing rights and the boundary between
New England and Acadia were pressing matters. The Board of Trade noted that
during Temple’s gubernatorial tenure, French ships were allowed to cure and
dry fish on the shore for a fee of £5. After Temple surrendered Nova Scotia at the
Treaty of Breda, the French continued that practice but had since claimed sole fish-
ing rights and captured English vessels as prizes. Equally distressing for the Board
of Trade was the French claim that the Kennebec should divide Acadia and New
England. They argued for the St Croix River, which the Board of Trade argued was
the “ancient” limit. To stretch Acadia even to the St George (which lies between the
Kennebec and Penobscot) was not permissible.56
   Unfortunately for the Board of Trade, officials in New England lamented that
French pales extended further than English officials might like admit. While certain
historical texts, such as Peter Heylyn’s Cosmographie, argued that French Acadia
extended only to the St Croix River, the de facto fictive boundary was farther west.57
Metropolitan officials were regularly reminded of this fact by the letters they re-
ceived from officials in the northeast. In 1699, the Board of Trade and Plantations
received a letter from Richard Coote, Earl of Bellomont, informing them that he
and the assembly in Massachusetts had passed an act to encourage trade with the
Abenaki, but he remained troubled by French Acadia. “I am sorry to say,” he la-
mented to the Board of Trade, that the St George River was the “boundary between
us and the French eastward.”58 Bellomont dispatched Colonel Wolfgang William
Romer, a military engineer whose loyalty to William the Prince of Orange had se-
cured him military posts in England and Ireland before his arrival in New York in
1697, to visit the region and report on what sorts of harbours he found and how
they might be fortified.59 The governor reported that the French were similarly sur-
veying Nova Scotia and the St John River with an eye to fortifying them.60
   For the English, fortifications were required to encourage settlements that would
lend weight to their territorial aspirations. Bellomont argued that without fortifica-
tions the coastal region John Nelson had suggested settling would remain empty of
English settlers, but noted that the governing men in Massachusetts had no desire
                             Neighbours in the Homeland                             25


to undertake such an operation. In this instance, a strong pale was weakening the
imperial fiction. “So long as they can sleep securely in this town of Boston,” the gov-
ernor concluded, “they [think] nor look no further.”61 Constructing forts in Maine
would have to be financed by the English government because local officials seemed
indifferent, and without fortifications the contested lands from the St George to the
Kennebec, the heart of the Dawnland, would remain under the control of the
Abenaki and, subsequently, more accessible to the French than the English.
   As war in Europe loomed, English and French officials became increasingly dis-
tracted and unable to address boundary issues in North America. Camile d’Hostun,
duc de Tallard, the French ambassador to England, wrote from London in February
1700 to recount a meeting held with the English commissioners charged with set-
tling American affairs. Tallard noted that the English were aware of French claims
to the coastline extending to the St George River, though the discussion focused
on fishing rights along several islands and whether anyone should be able to es-
tablish themselves in the region.62 Meanwhile, English officials continued to argue
for the St Croix River. Bellomont wrote to London from Boston with evidence to
support this claim. He had learned that the governor of Fort St John in Acadia had
been ordered by the French court to research Acadia’s ancient limits. The governor
had consulted with an elderly resident and learned that that St Croix was the true
boundary. According to Bellomont, a report with this information had been sent
to France.63 A few weeks earlier, however, the governor had received less encour-
aging news. Colonel Romer, who had been dispatched to survey the northeast
coast and its rivers towards Acadia, found several plantations around the river
St George deserted due to the previous war – and there was little timber suitable
for shipbuilding – but the adjoining coast offered extraordinary fishing. However,
“the French have taken the best part of it, which is from the River of Penobscott
to that of Ste. Croix.”64 Despite attempts at negotiations, denials of French rights,
and some diplomatic attention from English administrators, Acadia’s limits re-
mained undefined and the English were unable securely to access valuable lands
and fisheries in the Abenaki Dawnland. While the English and French recognized
the region’s strategic importance, neither could fully integrate the geographic fic-
tions of Acadia or Nova Scotia into their broader North American vision because
of European distractions, local disorganization, and, most important, the strength
of the Wabanaki, who could both define and defend their homeland.

                                      •••
As Europe descended into yet another war in 1701 – this time over whether Louis
XIV’s grandson would take the Spanish throne after the death of Charles II – what
little attention diplomats had paid Acadia since the peace of 1697 was directed
elsewhere. The War of the Spanish Succession left local officials in the northeast
to deal with the unfinished business of Acadia’s limits. But local officials were
no better equipped to deal with regional affairs than were imperial agents. Since
the English capture of Port Royal in 1690, the English and French spent as much
time competing among themselves as they did against each other. When offi-
cials in London and Paris considered the Acadian issue, they relied heavily on
information provided by administrators in the region, who were equally unsure
of who could claim what territory. Examining local confusion over boundaries,
26                            Homelands and Empires


competition among English and French inhabitants, and the lack of information
available to both sides helps explain why resolving imperial fictions in the north-
east remained such a difficult task.
   The Acadian coastline, let alone its western boundary with New England, was
something of a mystery to many French officials before the English capture of Port
Royal in 1690. Vincent Saccardy, the engineer general in Canada, complained of the
poor maps he was forced to use while in Acadia to rebuild the fortifications at Port
Royal. He had been charged by Governor Frontenac to travel from Chedabucto,
a fishing port near the Gut of Canso used by the French and English at different
times, to Port Royal, a task that caused him some trouble. He attempted to align
the coast he travelled with that plotted on poorly drawn maps he had been given,
causing him to draw his own charts.65 Saccardy’s task was a necessary one, because
before the English assault on Port Royal French officials had complained about the
region’s poor fortifications. Generally, New France was far too thinly populated,
with pockets of settlers spread out along the St Lawrence River. Acadia especially
was criticized “inasmuch as it has no fort of any value, and the settlers there are
scattered and dispersed.”66 Acadia needed new forts (ideally at LaHave) and set-
tlers who would pay more attention to fishing than trading whiskey and furs.67
   Fort building never amounted to much, and after Port Royal was captured lo-
cal administrators turned their attention to defining Acadia’s limits and worrying
about what the English might learn of New France’s geography. In a 1691 memoir,
New France’s intendant Jean Bochart de Champigny described the French pales
in North America. When he described Acadia, Champigny defined the region as
stretching to Pentagouët in the Dawnland and comprising a coastline rich in fish,
mussels, and seals. The intendant recorded that the English and Spanish also fished
along the coast, noting that the French Crown benefitted least of the three because
it lacked the ships necessary to protect its commerce.68 Equally important was the
rich knowledge of coastal passages, inlets, and local rivers garnered by the New
England merchants and fishermen who plied the Atlantic seaboard into Acadian
waters. For example, local officials considered the Boston merchant and Board of
Trade informant John Nelson a threat because he understood the French govern-
ment and had travelled extensively in New France. Upon his arrival in France as a
prisoner, Nelson was transferred to the royal prison at Chateau d’Angoulême, and
his guards were instructed to watch him carefully and prevent him from having
any communication with anyone. He was, according to the minister of the Marine,
“un homme dangereux.”69
   More important than preventing the English from spreading knowledge about
Acadia was collecting information and developing a strategy for governing the
region, even if the English technically held Port Royal. Mapping, measuring, and
describing Acadia was a thoroughly local affair that depended on whether a per-
son with the necessary skills was available to work on a project so necessary to
connecting pales and expanding French influence further into Indigenous home-
lands. French governors were fortunate that Jean-Baptiste-Louis Franquelin was
a capable surveyor and mapmaker. Franquelin arrived in Quebec in 1671 and be-
gan providing maps to accompany official correspondence in 1674, a task he con-
tinued into the 1690s. In 1685 Franquelin was charged with teaching navigation
in Quebec, a position he reluctantly accepted. He continued making maps and
                            Neighbours in the Homeland                             27


charts, and hoped that his services would bring more lucrative posts. When the
French court requested a map outlining the boundary between New France and
New England in 1688, Franquelin paid his own passage to France to request an in-
creased salary. He presented Louis XIV with his map and a petition requesting an
engineer’s post, which the sympathetic king granted.70 After his return to Quebec,
Franquelin spent much of his time preparing maps of the Atlantic coast and work-
ing with Frontenac on plans to attack New England and New York. His services
were obviously appreciated, as he spent much of the 1690s in Paris working with
the famous military engineer Sébastien Le Prestre de Vauban.71
   Franquelin worked to flesh out the geographic fiction of Acadia in the hopes that
it could become governable. In 1695, when the English still claimed Acadia as part
of the 1691 Massachusetts charter, a French memorial suggested that Acadia should
be divided in two to facilitate administration and military protection. Harkening
back to the days when Charles de Saint-Étienne de la Tour and Isaac de Razilly
claimed separate sections of the peninsula and mainland, Acadia would be split
into eastern and western regions.72 The western section would run along the coast
from Beaubassin to Kennebec, evidence that France maintained the latter river
as the boundary between Acadia and New England. It would be necessary to re-­
establish the fort at the Saint John River, which would attract settlers who could
be confident in the region’s safety. This fort would be staffed with a governor, a
major, and sixty soldiers.73 The eastern part of Acadia would include the peninsula
and about 100 leagues of coastline. Instead of using Port Royal as the capital, this
memoir suggested building a fort at LaHave, which would be outfitted like that on
the Saint John. Because English pirates were a constant threat to coastal settlements,
a patrolling frigate would be necessary for security. To ensure the region became
productive, any inhabitant who failed to meet the requirements of his concession
within two years would have his land grants revoked.74 Land management was
not only about building forts and maintaining soldiers, but also depended on the
improvements required of residents who would inhabit these new pales.
   While French officials hoped to divide Acadia, English politicians were so con-
fident that the geographic fiction of Nova Scotia fell under their possession that
they hoped to include it in an administrative reorganization. John Usher, lieutenant
governor of New Hampshire, informed the Board of Trade in 1693 that the “loyal
subjects” of Massachusetts had requested that all governments from Connecticut
to Nova Scotia be put under a single governor general to ease the unrest that had
been caused by division between those loyal to the Royal Government and those
who favoured the charter constitution.75 Putting the colonies on a sounder admin-
istrative footing was necessary because of the divisions in English America caused
by the Glorious Revolution of 1688. Governors and councillors loyal to the Catholic
James II found themselves faced with a restless population worried about the im-
pact of William and Mary’s accession in England. In 1689, Boston had revolted,
and imprisoned Governor Edmund Andros, who was subsequently sent back to
England. After the Boston Revolt of 1689, colonists differed on how best to gov-
ern the colony. Some wanted a return to the charter government and the “Puritan
church-state nexus” taken from them in 1684, while others wanted a more inclusive
administration.76 The result was the appointment of Sir William Phips as a royal
governor and the creation of the 1691 charter that extended Massachusetts along
28                            Homelands and Empires


the northeast coast to include the putative colony of Maine and the long-­imagined
Nova Scotia. After years of disorganized attacks on French America, English col-
onists in Massachusetts seemed ready to surrender their fear of political central-
ization and welcomed a strong imperial presence.77 While the French hoped to
reorganize Acadia to protect the region from the English, leaders in Massachusetts
wanted to include Nova Scotia in their reorganization to limit internal conflict.
   An important part of claiming Nova Scotia or Acadia was exercising authority
over the non-Indigenous peoples who lived in the region. For both the French
and English governments, this was a task that required careful oversight of land
claims and competition among settlers. While there are many examples of in-
habitants with competing claims to the same territory, or who failed to fully
comply with the obligations demanded of their particular grant, there are single
cases that help illuminate the importance of local land management. For New
France officials, the Damours brothers in Acadia caused concern by claiming land
to which they had no title and refusing to improve lands they were granted. In
Massachusetts, John Nelson and William Crowne (aided by his son, John Crowne)
launched a lengthy battle over the rights to Sir Thomas Temple’s Nova Scotia
which both frustrated officials while simultaneously sustaining New England’s
claim to the northeast. Imperial battles over geographic fictions were important,
but so too were the local challenges of administering the daily life of European
settlers living in Indigenous homelands.
   In Acadia, tensions over land can be analysed through the relationship between
Governor Villebon and the Damours family. Accusations of illicit trade, neglect-
ed land grants, and abuses of power levelled by the governor against the settlers
(charges that many settlers also levelled against the governor) demonstrate the
links between geographic control and expressions of administrative authority. The
Damours patriarch, Mathieu Damours de Chauffours, came to Quebec in 1651 and
was a member of the Sovereign Council from its creation in 1663 until his death
in 1695. Damours had noble ancestry, acquired land in Quebec, and established
the family precedent of using grants to engage in trade, which attracted the atten-
tion of government officials.78 Mathieu Damours and his wife had fifteen children,
several of whom established themselves in Acadia. Two in particular, Mathieu
Damours de Freneuse and René Damours de Clignancourt, received seigneurial
grants along the Saint John River and found themselves accused of mismanaging
their land. Mathieu’s 1684 grant included a stretch of territory from Jemseg to
Nashwaak. His seigneury was exceptionally well cultivated: he found settlers,
built a lumber mill, erected a house and barns, raised livestock, grew crops, and be-
gan trading with the Wulstukwiuk and Passamaquoddy.79 René, on the other hand,
seemed less interested in transforming his grant into an established seigneury. His
grant stretched from Meductic to Grand Falls, and in twelve years René cleared
only fifteen acres. He preferred to trade with the Indigenous peoples at Meductic,
which was the largest village on the Saint John.80
   The conflict between Villebon and the Damours family demonstrated how prop-
erty rights shaped the tensions between settlers within a pale and administrators
who hoped to govern them. Villebon, who seems to have been involved in trade
for personal financial gain, saw the Damours as a potential threat. Though he was
quick to chastise the Damours brothers for what he claimed was illicit behaviour,
                            Neighbours in the Homeland                            29


his only recourse was to threaten the family with losing their land on a likely
trumped up charge that the Damours family had not met their grant obligations.81
In addition to Mathieu and René, Louis Damours de Chauffours (the eldest son)
moved to the Saint John, likely to be close to his brothers. He cultivated land but
also established a post to trade furs and likely liquor with the Wulstukwiuk.82 By
1695, official complaints regarding “les sieurs Damours, frères,” were crossing the
Atlantic from Acadia to Versailles and initially the king seemed convinced that the
matter required attention. One of Villebon’s memoirs outlined the grants “claimed”
by the three brothers with a tone suggesting collusion or some kind of malfea-
sance, and concluded with a description of a fourth brother, named Depleine,
who “court le bois tout l’hiver avec les sauvages sans s’atacher a rien qu’a mener
une ‘vie’ libertine et semblable aux sauvage.”83 This kind of behaviour did little to
help develop the area or attract other settlers, which a memorial from the king to
Governor Frontenac and Intendant Champigny stressed was the region’s primary
goal. Because this part of the northeast was perceived to include some of the finest
land in North America, the king informed his colonial governors that the Damours
brothers were to be encouraged to establish themselves more thoroughly for the
benefit of the settlement.84
   After some investigation, officials at Quebec learned that the Damours brothers
were far from libertine layabouts. Champigny informed the minister of the Marine,
“the sons of the sieur d’Amours … who are settled on the River St. John, apply
themselves chiefly to cultivating their lands and raising cattle.”85 The English cap-
tive John Gyles, who spent years living with the Wulstukwiuk before spending
three years as a servant to Louis Damours, described the fine wheat field his mas-
ter cultivated.86 For reasons that likely had more to do with trade than cultivation,
Villebon wanted the Damours removed. But his best tactic to effect such a removal
depended on illustrating the dangers of failing to control and improve the land.
Since this part of Wulstukwik fell within both French and English imperial geo-
graphic fictions, territorial control as demonstrated by agricultural improvement
not only differentiated French farmers from their Indigenous neighbours, but also
employed expressions of sovereignty that competing European powers would
recognize as legitimate. That Villebon could only issue threats demonstrates the
limits of local authority to implement fully royal claims to territory.87
   Nevertheless, complaints about untended lands forced the French government
to address Acadia’s shortcomings. By 1699, Louis XIV was planning a complete
overhaul of Acadian land grants, frustrated by the fact that so few settlers seemed
to be undertaking the necessary improvements. Worse, it had reached the king’s
ears that Acadians were both trading with the English at Boston and allowing
New England merchants to travel to and trade within the limits of Acadia. Con-
sequently, the king required that all concessionaires who claimed lands by grant,
succession, sale, cession, or otherwise, present their titles or authentic copies for
whatever lands, ports, harbours, or rivers to which they laid claim on pain of
having those concessions declared null and void.88 Though the new legislation
was not aimed explicitly at the Damours brothers, their case crystallizes the chal-
lenges of monitoring settlements, planning for growth, and combatting illicit
trade in an Indigenous homeland desired by both the English and French. Exter-
nal threats and internal competition were viewed through the prism of borders
30                             Homelands and Empires


and geographic control. A memoir published in Acadia a year after the king’s
demand for proof of concessions illustrated just how unruly the entire granting
process had become. As the number of settlers in Acadia increased, so too did the
variety of the grants. Some arrived with concessions from the king, and others
had plots assigned to them from proprietary companies who often battled among
themselves for monopolies over fishing, furs, or trade. However, the memoir
noted that the variety of these types of grants did not preclude the conditions
attached to them, and in many cases those conditions had gone unfulfilled. It
was time, then, to reorganize land concessions.89 This was a Sisyphean task, as
administrators in Acadia soon learned. By 1701, Acadian officials were weeding
through claims and counterclaims that dated back to the conflicts among Charles
de Menou d’Aulnay, Razilly, and La Tour.90 The matter was never fully addressed,
however, as within a matter of months efforts shifted from monitoring internal
boundaries to preparing a military strategy against New England.
   Like their counterparts in Acadia and France, administrators in New England
and London spent the years before the outbreak of the War of the Spanish Succes-
sion sifting through competing land claims in the northeast. The capture of Port
Royal in 1690 and the new Massachusetts Charter of 1691 breathed new life into
decades-old claims to the newly acquired territory. These claims dated from the
close of the English Civil War when Sir Thomas Temple used his family’s con-
nections to Oliver Cromwell, 1st Lord Protector of the Commonwealth, to secure
for himself and William Crowne a grant for “the Contrie and Territories called
Laccady and that part of the Country Called Nova Scotia.”91 The territory was di-
vided, but the men often argued over boundaries. In 1659, Crowne leased his share
to Temple for £440 annually for four years. The Restoration of Charles II in 1660
brought more trouble for Temple, as new claimants to the territory (based on Sir
William Alexander’s 1628 grant from James I/VI) presented petitions to the king.92
Temple was appointed governor of Nova Scotia in 1662, but he was forced to pay
his rival £600 each year for the privilege.93 Temple and Crowne continued to argue
over the rights to Nova Scotia until the Treaty of Breda in 1667, which returned the
region to France. Charles II returned Acadia in explicit terms at Breda, noting that
France would regain control of Forts Pentagouët, St. John, Port Royal, LaHave,
and Cape Sable, “which said most Christian King did formerly enjoy.”94
   When Temple reluctantly returned his territories to France, the battle over propri-
etary rights shifted to calls for compensation for losses. By the 1690s, this cause had
been taken up by John Nelson (Temple’s nephew) and John Crowne (William
Crowne’s son and successful British playwright).95 During the few peaceful
years between the Treaty of Ryswick and the outbreak of the War of the Spanish
Succession, Nelson and Crowne petitioned the Board of Trade to have their claims
recognized and, more important, indemnified. Nelson’s interest in Acadia had
deep roots and was directly related to his commercial ties in the region. In fact, one
of Nelson’s first letters after learning in 1689 of the Glorious Revolution (news ar-
rived later in New England than almost all other colonies) was to Louis Damours
de Chauffours.96 In April of 1697, after learning that negotiations to end the Nine
Years’ War were “on foot,” Nelson presented his claim to the Board of Trade and
requested that his petition be laid before the king. As Temple’s nephew and the
beneficiary of his American holdings, Nelson reminded the board that his uncle
                            Neighbours in the Homeland                            31


had spent over £16,000 fortifying and improving his grant before it was restored to
France in 1667 “without examination or notice to the parties concerned.”97 Nelson
considered his request urgent, as he hoped to have the matter taken under con-
sideration while the English held the land (which they had captured during the
war). Five days after the Board of Trade and Plantations received Nelson’s peti-
tion, John Crowne presented his case. After laying out his claim’s provenance (Sir
William Alexander made over his lands to Charles La Tour, who in turn trans-
ferred the deed to Temple and William Crowne), Crowne argued that Charles II
had returned Acadia to France at Breda without compensating the proprietors.
Unlike Nelson, who asked that his petition be presented to the king, Crowne re-
quested that a clause be inserted into Lord Bellomont’s gubernatorial instructions
for Massachusetts to encourage the governor to facilitate the recovery of Crowne’s
estate. Such assistance would, according to Crowne, greatly assist his efforts to
have planters settle in the region.98
   The Council’s response in this matter demonstrated the confusion over bound-
aries and regional authority. Nelson seemed to be in an advantageous position,
given that in addition to his claim to the region he was also requested to provide
evidence that the English crown had a rightful title to the territory in question. He
provided a brief account of that title in April 1697, supported by a 1691 deed of
submission signed by sixteen French inhabitants from Chignecto after Phips’s raid
on the fort.99 The following October, Council member William Popple requested
additional information from Nelson on England’s claim to Nova Scotia, presum-
ably to help in the negotiations for the Peace of Ryswick. He sent a response in
early November.100 Nelson had become a perfect imperial agent, which members
of the Council recognized and exploited. His knowledge of Nova Scotia’s history
and boundaries, coupled with his personal stake in securing English title to the
region (which was presumably a prerequisite for his quest for title and compensa-
tion), ensured that he would do the Council’s bidding while they sorted out impe-
rial issues with France and delayed ruling on the Nelson-Crowne claims.
   Crowne received an equally equivocal response. Because he had requested that
his claim be included in a clause of Lord Bellomont’s gubernatorial instructions,
the Council was able to delay and avoid ruling; two days after Crowne’s petition
was read, the Council simply ruled that Nova Scotia was not in Bellomont’s com-
mission.101 Perhaps that was the case in April, but things seem to have changed
a few weeks later. When Bellomont’s commission was granted on 18 June, the
boundaries of his territory were set using the 1691 Charter of Massachusetts, thus
included “Massachusetts, New Plymouth, Maine, Acadie or Nova Scotia, and the
territory lying between Nova Scotia and Maine.”102 By September, England and
France had struck the Treaty of Ryswick, which failed to mention Acadia specifi-
cally but stated generally that “the Lord King of Great Britain shall restore to
the Lord the Most Christian King all the regions, islands, citadels and colonies,
wheresoever situated, which the French possessed before the present war was
declared.”103 Crowne, however, mounted one last attempt to reclaim his father’s
grant. In January 1698 he presented a memorial concerning the English title to
Penobscot that detailed the nefarious and underhanded treatment his father had
received at the hands of Sir Thomas Temple. John Crowne argued that Nova Scotia
and the Penobscot were two different regions, both of which had fallen to the
32                             Homelands and Empires


English in the 1650s, and, importantly, that the Penobscot should never have been
ceded to the French as part of Nova Scotia or Acadia.
   These detailed land claims served an important imperial purpose: claims to ter-
ritory within the geographic fictions of Nova Scotia and Acadia created an evi-
dentiary base with which European powers could further imperial negotiation for
lands that remained firmly under the control of their Indigenous inhabitants. John
Crowne and John Nelson, wittingly or not, became tutors in the geography and
history of the northeast. Crowne didn’t learn the fate of his petition until 1701, and
even then nothing was resolved. In October 1700, the Board of Trade requested
that John Crowne clarify the boundaries between Nova Scotia and the Penobscot
because his earlier petitions were contradictory.104 After further investigation, the
Council wrote to the king in late January 1701 arguing that Crowne had title to
the region but not suggesting any recompense. It found that the country of the
Penobscot, lying southwest of the St Croix River, which was considered the bound-
ary between Nova Scotia and New England, was held by William Crowne and
subsequently descended to John Crowne upon his death. However, that region
had been surrendered to France at the Treaty of Breda without compensation from
the king – though it never belonged to Nova Scotia or Acadia – and had remained
in dispute ever since, thus denying the petitioner from taking any benefit from the
land.105 This admission was all the satisfaction Crowne would receive; his title was
never reinstated. Crowne, a playwrite too long distracted by property matters,
published his final play, Caligula, three years before the decision, and in dedicat-
ing it to the king included a thinly veiled plea for support in his quest: to “our
wise and valliant king, who was born to free and do justice to opprest mankind:
and I hope to myself.”106 Crowne never received the justice he longed for. Having
suffered a “tedious illness” since 1687, the playwright received only £50 a year
from the Crown until his death in 1712.107 The following year, Nova Scotia/Acadia
was returned to Britain for good at the Treaty of Utrecht, though its boundaries
remained intentionally undefined. Nelson and Crowne’s efforts brought no imme-
diate results, but they served to propel a feverish interest in Acadia’s boundaries
that would long outlive either of the claimants.
   The Treaty of Ryswick’s reference to the Treaty of Breda as a model for Acadia’s
boundaries begged the question: what were Acadia’s boundaries in 1667? Just as
important, in 1697 English and French officials were aware that peace would not
last long. As a result, both powers continued marshalling as much geographic evi-
dence as they could to support their claims. The acting governor of Massachusetts,
William Stoughton, wrote to Villebon in March 1698 and informed him that
many ships had arrived from England with news of the peace, which Stoughton
published in the province. He had also freed French prisoners and put them on
ships back to Port Royal.108 With official fighting terminated, both sides could
focus on establishing boundaries and preparing for another round of competing
territorial claims.
   A few months after learning of the peace, Villebon was investigating the differ-
ence between English and French boundary claims while also hinting at the impor-
tance of Indigenous allies, especially those who held strategic territory. Alliances
were not universally maintained, but rather were subject to the contexts of war and
were often contingent on the availability of funds. He noted “though the French
                            Neighbours in the Homeland                            33


have always claimed that the Kennebec River separated French and English lands,
the English have never wanted to agree and claim instead that the boundary is
the St. George River, which is five leagues closer to Acadia.”109 Villebon remained
confident, however, that the English would not move into this contested territory.
The river cut through the Dawnland, and the Abenaki remained enemies of the
English because two of their chiefs had been held at Boston. Villebon described
the Abenaki as little more than placeholders and assumed that the imagined Nova
Scotia or Acadia would eventually determine local boundaries. He argued that
these Indigenous allies “would be sufficient to prevent the English from establish-
ing themselves in the region until the limits have been settled in Europe.”110 The
Abenaki inhabiting this contested region were valuable allies, and French minis-
ters recognized their ability to defend the French and slow English expansion. As
a result, French officials developed a two-tiered Indigenous policy: groups such as
the Abenaki, who held strategic land and could be useful in the contest against the
English, were to receive presents and be encouraged to unite around important
centres such as Pentagouët; the Mi’kmaq and their immediate neighbours, on the
other hand, would no longer receive presents because the war with England had
ended and they were located in the more northerly (and less contested) part of
the Baye Française (Bay of Fundy).111 The French in Acadia were simply to con-
tinue encouraging the Mi’kmaq to be good Christians. Such a strategy would have
seemed laughable for the Indigenous residents of Mi’kma’ki, Wulstukwik, and the
Dawnland, who had their own reasons for defending their territory.
   Despite their Indigenous allies, French officials focused much of their effort be-
tween 1697 and 1701 on establishing Acadia’s boundaries, despite the fact that no
official agreements had taken place. Roland Barrin de La Galissonière, the lieuten-
ant-general of France’s naval forces (and father of Marquis de la Galissonière, who
would be governor of New France) received strict instructions in May 1698 that
if he encountered English ships fishing along the coast of Newfoundland he was
to offer no challenge. However, any ships met outside that stretch of coast (from
Cape Race to Bonavista) should be informed not to fish where they are. Ships
should be sent away, harassed if they return, but not damaged because the task
of settling limits had not yet been finished.112 That task had been in the offing for
months, but nothing had been done. Pontchartrain wrote to Frontenac in March
informing him of peace in Europe, and noting that plenipotentiaries had “agreed
on the nomination of commissioners on both sides to regulate the boundaries of
the possessions belonging to the two Nations of America,” but added “that how-
ever, cannot yet be executed.”113 The continued boundary uncertainty curtailed the
authority that regional administrators could express to each other from their pales.
A frosty exchange between Bellomont and Frontenac concerning the treatment of
Indigenous allies and Indigenous lands earned Bellomont a curt reply from New
France’s governor, who informed him that commissioners were appointed to re-
solve the boundary issue and “therefore, Sir, it appears to me that, before speaking
to me in the style you have done in your last letter … you ought to have waited the
decision of those Commissioners.”114 Of course, both men would have continued
waiting indefinitely, as a decision in the matter never arrived.
   Unable to depend on imperial officials to offer a clear delineation between French
and English geographic fictions, local French administrators began collecting
34                             Homelands and Empires


descriptions and geographic depictions to send to Paris. Administrators at Ver­
sailles were aware that they lacked sufficient information to make informed rul-
ings on the subject – which, given that the French were much better informed than
the English, helps explain why settling the issue was nearly impossible – and thus
solicited local officials for additional information.115 Villebon received a letter from
Pontchartrain expressing surprise that the memoirs the minister requested on the
limits of Acadia had not yet arrived. He suggested that there must be a repository
of this kind of information at Port Royal, not to mention the prevalence of elderly
Acadians whose own memories, or the stories they learned from their fathers,
would illuminate the origin and location of the earliest English establishments.
Put simply, information was available and it was up to local authorities to collect it
and dispatch it to France at the first opportunity.116 Meetings with the English com-
missioners were ongoing, and Pontchartrain hoped to make stronger arguments
for the French vision of Acadia.
   Local officials promptly searched for documents in Port Royal and collected
oral testimony from elderly Acadians. In the first task they were unsuccessful.
“There are no acts in any of the offices of Port Royal that speak to the colony’s
limits,” Villebon wrote to Versailles in the fall of 1699, as “the English took the
precaution of removing all of them when they were in control of the fort.”117
Without extant records, administrators turned to Acadians themselves for edifica-
tion. In July, officials deposed Sieur Teinville, an ensign who lived in the garrison
on the Saint John River. The previous summer, Teinville reported, he was at Minas
and heard Sieur d’Entremont refer to his early days in Acadia.118 Philippe Muis
d’Entremont – an early settler, king’s attorney, lieutenant-general, and first baron
of Pobomcoup – was a driving force behind the Acadian settlement because he
concerned himself with agricultural development in his settlement on the south-
ern part of the peninsula. He had received land from his childhood friend, Charles
de Sainte-Étienne de la Tour.119 At the time Teinville heard him speak, d’Entremont
would have been in his nineties. He declared that he had arrived in the province
with Monsieur La Tour in 1650 when Port Royal was a garrison of forty soldiers.
At that time, according to d’Entremont, there was no English fort on the St George
River, which the French claimed as part of Acadia.120 When a number of English
did attempt to establish themselves on the St George, the French burned their
habitations. Finally, though at that time the English occupied Pemaquid, there was
never any post or trading house at the Kennebec River.121 Villebon was fortunate
that Teinville had happened to encounter d’Entremont; wanting documentary
evidence, French officials were forced to rely on oral testimony from one of the
region’s oldest inhabitants.
   In the months before the outbreak of the War of the Spanish Succession, French
officials at Versailles examined the evidence sent from Acadia and found it less
than impressive; not even a map of the region could settle matters. Teinville’s de-
position seemed to underwhelm the minister of the Marine, though he dutifully
forwarded it along to the king’s commissioners, who had been tasked with regu-
lating the limits of Acadia. Pontchartrain succinctly summarized the general opin-
ion of the available evidence: “It had been desired,” the minister stated bluntly,
“that you might have sent something more positive.”122 More evidence arrived in
late 1700, when Claude-Sébastien de Villieu sent his map of the St George River to
                            Neighbours in the Homeland                           35


imperial officials. In 1694, Villieu had travelled to Pentagouët to disrupt English-
Indigenous treaties, and two years later he participated in the Abenaki attack on
Pemaquid. After being captured, held captive in Boston, and then released at the
request of Frontenac, Villieu travelled to France for a short period before return-
ing to Acadia and briefly leading the colony after the death of Villebon.123 Before
his death, Villebon had charged Villieu to make a map of the St George, and “he
made the most exact map he could” and sent a copy to Pontchartrain.124 The map
would have been helpful; eventually, the English and French commissioners made
a preliminary agreement that the St George would serve as the boundary between
Acadia and New England, presumably an acceptable compromise between the
Kennebec and Penobscot. But apparently the agreement was never finalized, and
by 1701 England and France were once again at war.125
   Like the French at Port Royal, the English at Boston spent most of the peaceful
years between the Treaty of Ryswick and the outbreak of war in 1701 worrying
about boundaries. With the Nelson and Crowne legal battles largely ended by the
peace of 1697, local and imperial officials turned to other sources, including the
region’s elderly inhabitants, for descriptions of the region’s landscape and limits.
Edward Randolph, a colonial administrator who helped implement and oversee
trade regulations in British America, wrote to the Board of Trade from New York
in May 1698 to inform them of French territorial encroachment. He had been en-
couraged to write by Mr Frederick Phillips, “one of the most ancient inhabitants of
New York and the greatest trader to Albany,” as well as by other merchants who
traded with the Five Nations.126 These men had informed Randolph that for years
French traders had been trading at the heads of English rivers from Penobscot to
South Carolina. Randolph himself believed that the Peace of Ryswick stipulated
that the French had to remove themselves from English land and return north
of the Penobscot, which had always been considered the dividing line between
each Crown’s presumed territories. But he concluded with a more advisory note,
venturing “to suggest that upon a treaty to adjust all differences between the two
Crowns the articles formerly made between them about the boundaries in these
western parts may be agreed upon and settled.”127
   The “western parts” of Acadia, stretching along the Bay of Fundy coast south
from Beaubassin and running inland via the headwaters of the region’s most
prominent rivers, including the Wulstukw/St John River and Kennebec, were
of much concern to imperial officials, who continued collecting information from
their American settlements. Colonel Romer’s report on the region contained little
in the way of good news. The French had the best parts of the coast between the
Penobscot and the St George, and former English settlements in the region had
been poorly built and easily destroyed by the French and Indigenous peoples
during the last war. Romer also focused on Indigenous military cruelty. “Having
taken an old Englishman” during the last war, he reported, “they roasted him alive
on a spit in sight of the English in garrison.”128 More fortifications were needed.
Additional pales would expand English influence in the region and allow settlers
to move deeper into the Indigenous homelands. The Board of Trade learned in
August 1701 of the arrival of French commander Jacques-François de Monbeton
de Brouillan in Acadia from Provincial Secretary Isaac Addington, who report-
ed that the fort on the Saint John River had been demolished and the garrison
36                            Homelands and Empires


relocated to Port Royal, which was being fortified “with all vigor.” “We are likely,”
Addington concluded, “to have a dangerous neighbour in case of war.”129 And
war would come. Boundary concerns were quickly replaced with military strate-
gies that would, the English hoped, remove the French from contested areas and
resolve by force what had been so elusive in times of peace. Despite English and
French claims, however, Acadia and Nova Scotia were geographic exaggerations
based on a few pockets of settlement. The peoples of the Wabanaki confederacy
remained the most powerful force in the northeast.

                                     •••
French and English officials on both sides of the Atlantic might have struggled to
explain, define, or defend the existence and extent of Acadia or Nova Scotia, but
the region’s Indigenous inhabitants remained quite sure of their territory and were
prepared to defend it whenever necessary. From 1690 to 1710, English and French
forces were not so much battling each other as they were competing for Indigenous
alliances in the region’s homelands.130 Claiming sovereignty over the northeast
was not possible for either European power because they lacked the population
and military strength to push out the members of the Wabanaki confederacy. With
sovereignty out of reach, imperial administrators hoped to gain access to the geo-
graphic networks established by the Abenaki, Wulstukwiuk, and the Mi’kmaq.
British and French military strategies relied heavily on Indigenous participation
and were driven by concerns over territory and land claims. Consequently, the
treaties that ended these conflicts – which not only ignored Indigenous territorial
rights but also asserted imperial authority over both Indigenous people and their
lands – were difficult to explain to members of the Wabanaki confederacy.131 The
demographic and military weaknesses of England and France in the northeast
meant that Indigenous forces and Indigenous boundaries shaped the competi-
tion for the imperial geographic fictions. Rather than pitting English Nova Scotia
against French Acadia, conflict in this era was over which imperial power would
win the most influence in the Dawnland, Wulstukwik, and Mi’kma’ki.
   Officials in Acadia and New France were well aware that their best hope at pre-
venting English expansion was maintaining their Indigenous alliances, but those
relationships could not be taken for granted. The French had a longer and more
cordial relationship with the Mi’kmaq and their allies than the English did, and the
French learned from their interactions that Indigenous alliances required constant
attention and renewal.132 In a letter from the Jesuit Father Carheil to the gover-
nor of New France, Carheil noted that the Huron and Outaouais were wavering
in their alliance primarily because they had witnessed French military losses
and worried that the French would be incapable of protecting them against their
English and Iroquois enemies. Carheil concluded that French officials should
realize that their Indigenous allies made rational decisions based on their best in-
terests. “From this it will be seen,” the Jesuit wrote, “that our savages are much
more enlightened than one thinks; and that it is very difficult to conceal from their
penetration anything in the course of affairs that may injure or serve their inter-
ests.”133 Frontenac took the danger of a weakening Indigenous alliance seriously
and wrote to the Outaouais from Quebec, the strongest French pale, to reassure
